@@2054696 <p> An ecologist is travelling yet again to one of the world 's most important seabird breeding grounds in Western Australia to try to uncover what impact the changing environment is having on the colonies . <p> Located about 80 kilometres off the coastal city of Geraldton , the Abrolhos Islands are home to millions of seabirds which travel from the northern and southern hemispheres to breed and nest on the uniquely tropical system of reefs . <p> For 23 years , Dr Chris Surman has been travelling to the islands to collect data on the seabirds . <p> He says the Abrolhos is home to the globe 's largest populations of some types of birds . <p> Dr Surman says the islands are attractive to an array of bird species because they act as a confluence between tropical warm waters and cooler southern waters . <p> " We have some Antarctica species there that are nesting at their most northern limits and we have tropical species breeding at their most southern point , " he said . <p> " So it 's internationally and nationally significant - @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that goes at the moment . " <p> Despite the significance of the site , the breeding success of many seabirds on the Abrolhos has been declining over recent decades . <p> Dr Surman , his partner Lisa and son Shae , are spending the Christmas holiday period at a number of islands within the network 's Pelsaert Group to research whether that decline is being driven by changes in the Leeuwin current . <p> The current carries the birds ' major food source in the form of tiny larval crustaceans . <p> When the Leeuwin current , which flows down the WA coast , is weaker , food supplies for the birds are greatly reduced and the birds tend to breed later in the season with less success . <h> Normal breeding period after a decade of disruption <p> During a trip to the Abrolhos earlier in the year , Dr Surman says it appeared that for the first time in about a decade , seabirds are breeding at a more ' normal ' period . <p> He says they are laying their eggs in August and September this year @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and December months . <p> " Which suggests that this year there seems to be a super-abundance of food supply , for the birds to get into breeding that early after a fairly late season last year , " he said . <p> Seabird expert and Conservation Council of WA volunteer , Nic Dunlop , has been studying the establishment of new and frontier colonies of tropical seabirds south of the Abrolhos for about 30 years . <p> Dr Dunlop says Dr Surman 's long-term data sets on the seabirds suggest that the acceleration in the establishment of those fresh colonies appears to be closely linked to birds searching for food . <p> " Because what forces seabirds to normally naturally go back to their own breeding areas , the areas in which they hatched to become breeders , that situation only tends to break down when the area that they come from no longer supports the same number of birds , " he said . <p> " So it forces the young , when they get to a breeding age , to disperse . " <p> Dr Dunlop says shifts @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ central west coast of WA , which impacts the seabirds . <p> " So there are climate change winners and losers in the suite of species we 're looking at , " he said . <p> He says seabirds are an indicator of the overall health of ecosystems like the sensitive Abrolhos Islands . <p> " Because they 're on top of the food chain they 're basically giving us a level of information about what 's happening below them , " he said . <p> " In terms of what 's happening to the plankton , what 's happening to the zooplankton , what 's happening to the small fishes ; all the things that eventually end up with birds being part of that system . " <h> Island visitors aware of significance of seabird colonies <p> Pia Boschetti is a pearl farmer at the Abrolhos Islands who spends about five months of the year living at the islands . <p> She says Dr Surman 's knowledge of the local seabirds has made many island users more aware of the significance of the site . <p> " There 's birds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ amounts , but it 's a rare breeding ground , one of only two in the world . " <p> Pia Boschetti <p> " Normally I only care about things that are under the water , but the birdlife , obviously above the water , he 's helped to show me that that 's exciting . " she said . <p> " It just blows me away because I 've been going to the islands since I was born and I 've never really paid much attention to the birds , other than the seagulls around and a few pretty birds . <p> " So I think about how he 's educated me and how great it is when I get to tell a story to someone on how important it is . <p> " There 's birds that we see over there that we think are in huge amounts , but it 's a rare breeding ground , one of only two in the world - which is something I 've found out from Chris . " <p> She says Dr Surman 's knowledge has helped improve the community 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's made me really aware of the impact that we can have on the islands , " she said . <p> " I do n't think anyone would have known how important the birds are until Chris and Lisa did their research . @@2054719 <h> Search smh : <h> Search in : <h> Overseas adoption : the case against <h> Ian Robinson <p> " When you make overseas adoption easy , this creates a market demand and , in a market economy , where there is a demand there will be people to meet it . For a price . " <p> Tony Abbott wants to reform our overseas adoption regulations to make it easy to bring babies into the country . <p> This stands in contrast with his policy of making it difficult for needy adults to get here . <p> The reason is not hard to see . Abbott believes in the market . And there is a big market for babies among the well off , those who are infertile or gay , or too old or too busy to use the normal reproductive methods to conceive a child . <p> Until the 1960s , this market for babies was met by convincing unwed mothers to part with their ' ' bastard ' ' children but this stopped as the stigma of being a ' ' fallen woman ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Advertisement <p> Then along came in vitro fertilisation ( IVF ) and donor conception ( DC ) and it seemed the childless could again get themselves a child . But IVF and DC have not lived up to their promise . The outcome is uncertain , expensive , demeaning , invasive and time-consuming . <p> Enter Abbott . ' ' Where there are children who legitimately need a safe and loving home , and where there are people who dream of being able to provide that home ' ' , the government will streamline arrangements ' ' to enable them to come together ' ' . <p> What his advisers have not told him is that overseas adoption is made difficult for good reason . <p> When you make overseas adoption easy , this creates a market demand and , in a market economy , where there is a demand there will be people to meet it . For a price . <p> The appearance of rich foreigners in Third World countries looking for ' ' orphans ' ' to adopt is an open invitation to unscrupulous criminals to supply @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the countries adopted children come from and the governments of these countries are often too poor , too disorganised and too corrupt to establish adequate mechanisms to guard against it . <p> The going price for a child to adopt can be tens of thousands of dollars . As a consequence , the practice of ' ' child laundering ' ' - forging false identities to create ' ' orphans ' ' for well-to-do foreigners - is not uncommon and is frequently undetected . <p> The international child adoption market can easily descend into an unscrupulous and inhuman trade in disadvantaged Third World children for the benefit of relatively well-off Westerners - a kind of reverse Robin Hood taking children from the poor and gives them to the rich . <p> Take the example of Guatemala : in 2006-07 , a United Nations International Children 's Emergency Fund survey found there were less than 400 babies under one year old in Guatemalan childcare institutions , yet US immigration figures showed 270 babies under one were being adopted into the US each month . Many of these were simply stolen from their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Guatemala suspended adoptions to the US for some years . <p> Proponents will argue that if proper controls are in place , everything will be legal and above board . Yet David Smolin , a law professor from Birmingham , Alabama , who adopted two children from an orphanage in India , disagrees . Despite taking every precaution to ensure the legality of the adoption , he later found out that his two children had been literally stolen from their mother . Smolin has been campaigning ever since for stricter regulation of inter-country adoption and believes until this happens the practice is too fraught to be safe . ' ' Streamlining arrangements ' ' will only exacerbate these already existing dangers . <p> Additionally , it is clear from research that adoption is not an unequivocal bed of roses : adoptees are over-represented in psychiatric institutions , divorce courts and jails . And these figures are much higher for inter-country adoptees . <p> And , as inter-country adoptees become adults , some have begun to find a voice and question the practice . For example , the New York adoptee website @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ What is fundamentally wrong with inter-country adoption is that white Westerners adopt children , while non-whites in non-Western countries relinquish and supply those children . ' ' <p> People who care about the best interests of mothers and children have long believed adoption should be discarded as a compassionate choice , except in a handful of extreme cases . <p> If people are serious about helping poor children in Third World countries , they can join one of the many support plans that are a fraction of the cost of adoption . <p> One naturally sympathises with couples who , due to factors beyond their control , are unable to have children of their own but they need to consider whether they want to satisfy their desire for children , no matter what the cost to the child . <p> Inter-country adoption creates a class of people who do not feel entirely comfortable in their adoptive country but have , at the same time , been alienated from the culture of their country of birth . Many are now literally without a home . <p> Ian Robinson is a Melbourne freelance @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> A nation 's greatest resource is its people and we should be wary of systematic or mass adoption . It 's a sensitive issue , but how much infertility is lifestyle-related i.e. due to couples leaving it all too late ? <p> Tony Abbott seems to have ' switch on , switch off ' humanity , with too much pandering to the well to do . Humanity means many things : compassion for the less fortunate and the less well off , respect for those not yet born , etc . The way asylum seekers are treated , the lack of respect for our natural environment and the lies on climate change - the biggest threat to all inhabitants of Australia - are a disgrace . We are not just nodding dummies here to observe the magnates getting richer , while so much we value gets trampled upon ! <p> " On Thursday the Australian Agency for International Development - AusAID - ceased to exist as an independent organisation . @@2055657 <p> Silicon Valley is a bit like the ancient city of Babylon . A confluence of the right geography , right timing , and the right mix in the melting pot allowed them both to thrive . <p> Even the mythological status is fitting . After all , it 's not easy to replicate what circumstance has brought together . <p> And yet , many are looking for the secret recipe to copy what Silicon Valley has achieved . But is it just a matter of understanding the recipe , picking the right scale , and being patient ? <p> There have been successful innovation hubs elsewhere and in disciplines outside of information technology . Take the Cambridge Science Park for instance . The site was donated to Trinity College in 1546 , but was n't developed until 1970 . It is now one of the leading innovation and research hubs in the world . <p> One imperative for the development of the Cambridge Science Park was a push from the then UK Government to improve returns on investment in basic research and higher education through increased technology @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with the current push in Australia to improve the connection between research and industry . <p> In Cambridge , it took a decade for the number of companies in the science park to grow to 25 . Initially being close to the world class research activity of Cambridge University was the pull . As a mini-cluster of technologies and talented people emerged in the 1980s it became attractive to more people and organisations , and so the effect snowballed . Venture capitalists and spin-outs joined the mix . <p> Importantly , there was also investment in accommodation and sporting facilities , conference and meeting places , and eating places . These facilities helped to develop the culture of the Cambridge Science Park . <p> In the 1990s , the number of companies grew to around 64 with larger and better funded companies present , and a focus on life sciences emerged . Some degree of evolution to the natural focus for the geographical area has been in the recipe for success in Cambridge . <p> There are also plenty of examples of less successful innovation clusters . The One-North Science Habitat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ better integration and private sector participation . It was built with further investment from the Singapore Government after the initial Singapore Science Park only achieved modest success . The Singapore Science Park was originally largely government directed in its set up with a low density design and no direct academic links . <p> The lessons from these examples are that culture is critical . Silicon Valley , Cambridge Science Park , and other successful examples of clusters have brought people together and actively facilitated interactions between them . <p> The benefits of this kind of collaboration extend beyond immediate economic growth . Close interactions give rise to new networks . New networks then facilitate the flow of expertise and knowledge between people and organisations which is essential for supporting future discoveries and breakthroughs . <p> Networks are important for the types of challenges we are facing . Think about climate change , biosecurity , and food security ; these span country , organisational and disciplinary boundaries . Collaboration across traditional disciplines and between sectors ( particularly between the research and industry sectors ) is an essential ingredient in developing solutions to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are the efficiency dividends from joining up the players , and lowering the costs of interacting . <p> Australia can learn from these international examples to improve its own collaborative effort . The 2013 Global Innovation Index placed Australia at 19 out of 142 countries , behind countries such New Zealand , Israel , the Republic of Korea and Ireland . <p> Two of the areas in which Australia performed poorly related to our ability to form clusters and export high technologies . Increasing collaboration and knowledge adoption are key areas that need to be improved if Australia is to remain globally competitive . <p> At the moment , however , we still do n't have anything that combines the right geography , the right scale and the right mix in the melting pot to get the broad and deep connections of a Silicon Valley or Cambridge Science Park . <p> There are certain areas in Australia were there is sufficient expertise and industry activity to build collaborative melting pots of global scale . We have natural focus points such as manufacturing in Melbourne , and resources in Perth . Taking @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ these strengths to improve collaboration . High density , shared facilities and direct links to academia would also improve flows between industry , research , and government . <p> With scale comes international visibility , easier access , greater efficiency of investment for public and private funds , and that magic that happens when people with different backgrounds collaborate . The critical part will be to get the culture right . <p> With patience we can bring research and industry together and improve our collaborative performance . CSIRO 's own Global Precincts aim to do just that by emphasising physical closeness , direct linkages , and active facilitation of collaboration . <p> Australia might not have a Silicon Valley , but these kinds of projects already underway , will help Australia compete in the increasingly global innovation system .

@@2056929 <h> Sydney Morning Herald columnist <p> A couple of mornings ago my wife , sitting in bed , said : ' ' Are we having banana boats ? ' ' . Having been married for some time I instantly deduced the actual meaning of this sentence . First , it was not a question . Second , it did not refer to ' ' we ' ' . Third , the clock was ticking . <p> I knew what had to be done . I would go downstairs , very soon , and make a pot of coffee , pour a jug of milk , make toast ( soy and linseed loaf ) , apply almond butter to the toast , then add a layer of sliced bananas . A banana boat . This would then be taken upstairs on a tray , where my wife would feign surprise . <p> The one thing that would not happen after hearing that question was that I would dally and , say , finish the paper . That had happened once . After about eight minutes from question to inaction @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ her own breakfast . Perhaps stomped is too strong a word but there had been a purposeful , unspoken impatience that I do not want to see again . Driven , no doubt , by hunger . <p> Advertisement <p> Thus I put aside the paper , or iPhone , or both , went downstairs , and made a banana boat in a timely , huff-avoiding manner , then delivered it all on a tray to my wife who feigned surprise . You could argue I am well trained . You could argue , if you knew the terrain of my life , that it is dominated by women . My kitchen cabinet is mostly women . My doctor is a woman , my dentist , my tax account , my Pilates instructor . <p> At work , the news director is a woman . The deputy news director is a woman . The director of all the Life Media websites is a woman . The manager of the Comment sections is a woman . The editor of the opinion page is a woman . The editor of The Sun-Herald is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ position because they are a woman but because they were the best qualified for the job . Technically , I work for all of them . <p> In fact , the majority of the journalists at the Herald are women . This is not going to change . The paper recently hired nine summer interns . Seven are women , because they had the best qualifications , test scores and interviews . <p> Journalism is being feminised . Not by quotas , but by talent and sheer weight of numbers and drive . The majority of graduates from journalism schools are women . The majority of all university graduates are women . The majority of top graduates are women . <p> This is not going to change , either . It is going to become more pronounced , because the majority of top high school graduates are women . Their advantage has become dominant , structural . In the 2013 Higher School Certificate , of the top achievers in English , 70 per cent were women . In cultural studies , 85 per cent were women . Of the 121 students @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 69 per cent . <p> In the most impressive group of all - the HSC All-rounders list - the 1217 students who , among the 74,000 who attempted the HSC in 2013 , were in the top band ( at least 90 per cent ) in five subjects , 715 were female and 502 were male . That is a 59 per cent preponderance of young women in the elite of the elite . At the Herald , my social media adviser , who has just turned 21 , was on the 2010 All-rounders list . Yet another woman . <p> This trend has been going on for many years but will become more pronounced as its impact becomes cumulative . Fifty years ago , according to the Australian Bureau of Statistics , the number of women aged 20 to 24 who were in further education was less than 27 per cent . Today it is 54 per cent . What a transformation in two generations ; the full ramifications are still building . The majority of graduates in a majority of the professions are women , including the once impregnable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , structural impediments that prevent this transformation from translating proportionately into leadership roles for women . The most obvious is child-bearing and rearing . It has a profound impact . Another impediment is the continued domination of men in economics , engineering , software design , information technology , mathematics and science . <p> Elsewhere the change is inexorable . We already live it at the Herald . @@2061809 <h> Disclosure statement <p> Ros Harvey works for Sense-T program at the University of Tasmania and is the Founding Director . The program receives funding from a range of sources including Australian and Tasmanian governments , the private sector and the Australian Research Council . <p> Big Data has a reputation for being creepy ; the domain of " Big Business " and " Big Government " . At best it 's the driver of relentless advertising , uniquely targeted and eerily reminiscent of our most recent internet searches . <p> At worst it heralds the Orwellian prophecy : Big Brother is watching you . Recent revelations by Edward Snowden have only fuelled suspicions that our data is being used in ways we do n't really comprehend and by people we do n't even know . <p> Yet Big Data could be one of the greatest tools we 've ever had to drive economic , social and environmental sustainability . <h> How big is Big ? <p> There is no concrete definition or agreed threshold upon which everyday data becomes Big Data . <p> The term was coined @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to describe the challenge of processing and analysing vast amounts of computer-generated information . <p> Since then , an explosion in technology including social media , smartphones and sensing devices have seen a corresponding explosion in the amount of data generated . People 's interaction with the internet has changed radically and the amount of personal information they disclose online has skyrocketed . <p> " ... big data refers to things one can do at a large scale , that can not be done at a smaller one , to extract new insights , or create new forms and value , in ways that change markets , organisations , the relationship between governments , citizens and more . " <p> Let 's just say we 're talking about more zeros and ones than you can possibly imagine ; data in different formats , from different types of technology , structured and unstructured . <p> According to Cisco futurist Dave Evans , by 2015 one Zettabyte of data will flow over the internet . That equals a stack of books from Earth to Pluto 20 times or 116 billion miles . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ moved away from collecting just the relevant bits of data we need to answer the important questions . <p> Now we can collect everything and see if any interesting questions emerge . It turns traditional research on its head . Rather than proposing a hypothesis and collecting sample information to test it , we scan and analyse information to identify a hypothesis . <p> Big Data harnesses data analytics , modelling and machine learning to complete infinite complex calculations that are beyond the capabilities of the human brain . <h> Big Data for sustainable development <p> In terms of sustainable development , Big Data offers us unparallelled opportunities to study the complex relationships between people , industries , economies and the environment . <p> It can help us identify previously unknown patterns and relationships and find more sustainable ways to use our resources . <p> Sense-T , spun out of the University of Tasmania and CSIRO , is analysing data and designing smartphone apps that help food and wine producers to predict pasture growth , minimise chemical use , identify disease risks and schedule irrigation . <p> Soil sensors can help @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be re-used and re-purposed , creating value on many different levels . For example , a dairy farmer can use data from soil sensors to help minimise fertiliser use . The same sensor data could be accessed by an oyster farmer to manage water quality , by science students to study the environment , or by government to help cut red tape . <p> It 's about sharing data and making it available in a way that helps people do more with the resources they have available . <p> This is increasingly vital in a world approaching nine billion people , with no more arable land , unprecedented climate change and a responsibility to help lift people out of poverty . <p> Other examples of Big Data innovations include software to predict bushfire risk , algorithms that give early warnings of disease outbreaks and modelling to demonstrate environmental impacts of climate change . <p> Big Data can be -- and is being -- used for good , social and environmental . It is not a silver bullet . It does not have all the answers and does not replace all traditional @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the most powerful weapons in the arsenal . <h> Making it less creepy <p> Unfortunately , Big Data has a creepy reputation because it 's inaccessible to most people and there is limited transparency about how data is used . <p> Privacy is not the entire issue . There is a lot of information people are happy to share ; often very personal information . The success of social media sites is predicated on our overwhelming urge to connect and share with others . <p> Trust , choice , consent , transparency and accountability are also big issues at play . Legal frameworks have so far struggled to address these issues , unable to adapt to the rapidly advancing world of technology and information . <p> There are technological and coding measures in development that show promise . For example , Sense-T is working with the Australian Bureau of Statistics to develop a complex combination of standard and unique statistical techniques to safeguard data . <p> But the real trick will be democratising Big Data in a way that gives people access to the benefits , while being open and transparent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ willing to share their information if it creates value for them and if it creates value for society and the environment we live in . <p> Organisations that can deliver value on both levels , while respecting people 's privacy and maintaining trust , will have a unique market proposition in the world of Big Data . 
@@2061848 <h> A 2014 calendar for climate change policy-making <h> Author <h> Disclosure statement <p> David Hodgkinson does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> To mark the beginning of a new year , I have put together a list of some of the major issues and events expected to influence climate change policy-making in 2014 . From 1 to 8 , these are my top predictions . <h> 1 . Countries struggle to reach agreement on mitigation targets <p> In 2011 , nations participating in the Durban climate change conference agreed " to develop a protocol , another legal instrument or an agreed outcome with legal force " on emissions reduction targets applicable to both developed and developing states . <p> Then , in 2012 , it was agreed that " elements " of a draft negotiating text for such a document would be " considered " no later than the end of 2014 , " with a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for agreement later that year and then implementation in 2020 . <p> Last year , at Warsaw , a loose time-frame was set for countries to propose their " intended nationally determined contributions " to the 2015 agreement -- the end of the first quarter of 2015 for those " ready to do so " . <p> Nations are now unsure how to fulfill these proposed contributions , and do n't have plans to deliver on them . Overall , states are n't ready to collectively address the climate change problem . <h> 2 . Scientists agree that the planet has a carbon budget <p> The September publication of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change 's Fifth Assessment Report on the physical science basis for climate change referred for the first time to a global cumulative carbon budget . The IPCC found that to hold global warming to 2C above pre-industrial levels ( the limit agreed by most states as a global warming " safety threshold " ) , total emissions can not exceed one trillion tonnes of carbon . <p> By 2011 , 515 gigatons ( more than half of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The Trillionth Tonne website continually updates estimates of current total cumulative emissions from fossil fuel use , cement production and land-use change since industrialisation began at about 575 gigatons . Based on emission trends over the past 20 years , it expects the trillionth tonne will be emitted in October 2040. <h> 3 . Four measures may stop emissions growth <p> In June this year , the International Energy Agency ( IEA ) produced a report outlining energy measures to slow the impacts of climate change . The IEA 's chief economist Fatih Birol summarises and ranks these measures as : <p> adopting specific energy efficiency measures <p> limiting the construction and use of the least-efficient coal-fired power plants <p> limiting methane emissions from upstream oil and gas production <p> increasing the phase-out of fossil fuel subsidies . <p> Oil , gas and coal producers received more than US$500 billion in government subsidies in 2011 . These financial contributions in turn subsidise activities which " are pushing the world towards dangerous climate change " , beyond the 2C threshold . <h> 4 . We realise dangerous climate change is on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A paper published this month by James Hansen , Jeffrey Sachs and others argues that cumulative emissions of a trillion tonnes of carbon ( associated with the 2 degrees target ) would spur eventual warming of 3 to 4 degrees . <p> Such warming , they say , would have disastrous consequences . Put another way , such a target is " well into the range of dangerous human-made interference with climate . " <p> Natural gas is merely a gangplank to cleaner energy , not a **27;195;TOOLONG <h> 5 . Natural gas : a gangplank to a cleaner energy future , not a bridge <p> I have argued previously that industry sectoral agreements might be easier to make than international climate change agreements . Also , the advantages of an LNG sectoral agreement could be environmentally effective . By displacing coal with gas emissions will be reduced . <p> But there are questions associated with a natural gas " bridge " to a carbon-free future . <p> While cleaner , natural gas is a fossil fuel and is mostly methane -- a potent greenhouse gas . Furthermore , a recent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( natural gas ) are likely double that cited in existing studies . And , as Joe Romm notes , natural gas as a bridge fuel must displace coal only , rather than a combination of coal and renewable energy sources . <h> 6 . ' Common but differentiated responsibilities ' may be dead <p> Underpinning the international climate change regime is the principle that states have common but differentiated responsibilities ( and respective capabilities ) in addressing the climate change problem . It 's more or less the " polluter pays " principle , with developed states given responsibility to take the lead . So , under the Kyoto Protocol , only developed states have emission reduction targets . <p> Developed and developing states have now agreed to agree on an agreement from 2020 , under which both will have targets . <p> The imperative of bringing all major emitters into a regime of climate commitments is clear . There is simply no other way to head off the coming crisis ... just do the math . <p> Nonetheless , at the Warsaw talks China clearly emphasised the importance of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Two-thirds of cumulative carbon dioxide and methane emissions can be attributed to 90 international entitiesAAP <h> 7 . Emissions responsibility is attributed <p> A recent study shows that 63% of cumulative global carbon dioxide and methane emissions between 1751 and 2010 can be traced to 90 international entities . Half of these emissions have been emitted since 1984 . <p> This study importantly offers a different view of climate change responsibility : major producers of fossil fuels are not all located in developed states . <p> Further , in tracing emissions to major carbon producers -- corporate entities -- it opens new opportunities for such entities " to become part of the solution rather than passive ( and profitable ) bystanders to continued climate disruption . " <h> 8 . Future generations in the " climate casino " <p> Climate change is the most significant threat to humanity and the natural world . <p> Yale economist William Nordhaus refers to humanity entering the " climate casino " . Economic growth is producing perilous changes in the climate and earth systems , leading to unforeseeable and dangerous consequences . Humanity is rolling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We benefit now from carbon emissions , but the costs will be borne by future generations . And the present benefits are often modest , the future costs severe . <p> The Nobel laureate Paul Krugman , in his review of Nordhaus ' climate casino book , notes that Nordhaus gives relatively little weight to the interests of future people -- he uses a high " discount rate " . <p> Nicholas Stern , however , with a low discount rate , asks the present generation to make urgent sacrifices for the sake of future generations . <p> The prevailing view at the international mitigation level seems to be , as the saying goes , " Why should I care about future generations ? What have they ever done for me ? " <p> Our view about future generations , however , will determine climate change policy in 2014 and beyond . <p> Happy new year . <p> This article included contributions from Rebecca Johnston , a corporate lawyer for a major law firm . 
@@2063135 <h> We live in the best of times , so do n't buy the media gloom <h> Tom Switzer <p> Unfashionable though it is to say so , we are living in a golden age . For never has there been less hunger and disease and more security and prosperity around the world . That is why my London Spectator colleagues and I think 2013 has been the greatest year in the history of the world . <p> The media and intellectual class feed us a constant diet of doom and gloom - predicting anything from global famine to climate catastrophe to energy crisis . Of course , scepticism is useful in times when opinion leaders are under the spell of various fallacies . The Arab world that confronts us today , for instance , is not the one announced in the Pollyannaish program three years ago . Words like ' ' chaos ' ' and ' ' violence ' ' are freely used to describe it . <p> All the Cassandras notwithstanding , the sky is not falling . A cold , dispassionate look at the big @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has never been better . <p> Start with poverty : In 2000 , the United Nations announced several Millennium Development Goals . One was to halve the number of people in extreme poverty by 2015 . That target was met - to too little media fanfare - in 2008 . Another goal was to improve the lives of 100 million slum dwellers - with sanitation , water supply and better housing - by 2020 . That target was met 10 years early . <p> Advertisement <p> In China , thanks to capitalism , people are being lifted out of poverty at the fastest rate ever recorded . In the early 1980s , there was more than 80 per cent living in extreme poverty . Today , it 's 10 per cent . True , China remains far poorer than advanced nations - and it faces demographic challenges - but the economic gap between East and West is closing fast . <p> Kishore Mahbubani , a distinguished Singaporean author-diplomat , revealed this year that in 1990 , 1 billion people earned enough income to consider making discretionary purchases beyond mere necessity . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Take health : Advances in medicine , and in the ability to produce affordable drugs for millions of people , mean we are living longer and reaching goals that once seemed outlandish . In the past decade , the average life expectancy in Africa increased from 50 to 55 ; and the number of people dying from AIDS and malaria has declined dramatically . <p> Take energy : the explosion of wealth and population , far from inducing an energy crisis and ruining the environment , has coincided with a decline in fossil fuel consumption in the rich world . According to the US Energy Information Administration , US energy-related greenhouse gas emissions declined nearly 4 per cent last year ( as the economy grew nearly 3 per cent ) . And according to The Wall Street Journal , this has brought US emissions to their lowest level in two decades . <p> This extraordinary ( and , again , under reported ) achievement has hardly anything to do with carbon taxes or wind farms and almost everything to do with consumer demand for more efficient cars and factories as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ natural gas from oil shale . <p> We live in an age of energy abundance . Since the oil embargo in 1973 , the Americans have been huge net importers , and presidents and lawmakers have proposed solutions to their energy problem . Today the US , thanks to impressive breakthroughs in ' ' fracking ' ' technology , is on the cusp of being energy independent . And , it was revealed this year , Britain has enough shale gas to power itself for the next half century . <p> Take the environment : advanced nations have set higher standards , which in turn have contributed to cleaner air , land and water . Although natural disasters , such as the recent NSW bushfires , still wreak havoc , the speed of the recovery efforts showed remarkable resilience . Humans can not control the weather . But as nations grow richer , we will better handle damage wrought by nature . <p> Then there is security . Visions have changed in two decades : The Global Convergence , the title of Mahbubani 's widely acclaimed book , has replaced The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . And for good reason : fewer people are dying in wars than ever before . According to Mahbubani , in the last quarter century the number of wars that kill at least 1000 people a year has declined by 78 per cent . <p> Still not convinced these are the best of times for the West and the rest ? Mahbubani reminds us that 30 years ago , the world 's fastest computer , the Cray-2 , the size of a washing machine , was exorbitantly priced and needed coolants to avoid overheating . Today , its match is the iPad 2 , a tiny device by comparison that runs on 10 watts of power . <p> This golden age has happened , because the world is embracing capitalism , and its people are more interdependent through trade as never before . No doubt there will be plenty of bumps along the way . But as 2013 comes to an end , one can not help but think Harold Macmillan was ultimately right when he declared : ' ' We 've never had it so good . @@2063157 <h> Authors <h> Disclosure statement <p> Jason Halford works for the Seeds for Life Project - the Queensland partner of the Australian Seed Bank Partnership - as the principal seed collector and seed technologist . <p> Kat Grigg does not work for , consult , own shares in or receive funding from any company or organization that would benefit from this article , and has disclosed no relevant affiliations beyond the academic appointment above . <p> In 1926 , just outside of St Petersburg in Russia , botanist and geneticist Nikolai Vavilov set up the Pavlovsk Experimental Station . It was one of the world 's first " seed banks " . <p> The term " seed bank " or " gene bank " describes a place where seeds are stored , such as food crops or rare plants . Why store seeds ? We depend on plants for things like food , construction , medicine , clothing , soil and oxygen . Many plants are now classed as rare or threatened and storing seeds is the only way to ensure their survival . <p> During the 28 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at the Pavlovsk Experimental station boxed up various seeds , moved them to the basement , and took shifts protecting them from the German army . A number of scientists even ended up dying before the end of the siege in 1944 , choosing to starve rather that eat the seeds . <p> The station collection now houses more than 100 varieties each of gooseberries , raspberries and cherries , and over 1000 varieties of strawberries . In fact 90% of this collection exists in no other research collection anywhere else in the world . As most of these can not germinate from seed , they are stored as plants in the ground . <h> Why do we need seed banks ? <p> The Millennium Ecosystem Assessment estimates between 60,000-100,000 plant species are threatened with extinction- that 's around one quarter of all known plants . The biggest threats to plants now are land use change , over exploitation , and climate change . <p> The demand for food is predicted to rise dramatically in the next few decades as population increases . Currently we depend on fewer than 12 of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the food we eat . Natural disasters or disease outbreaks are a real threat to existing food crops and having seeds stored away is an insurance policy against these threats . <p> " Resilience " is stronger when we have a diversity of plants rather than monocultures . With monocultures , if one plant gets sick , disease is more easily spread to all of the other same types of plants . This is especially true in commercially grown crops used in agriculture or in restoration works for a natural ecosystem . <p> Certain traits of a plant and a plant 's DNA may also prove useful in the future . In western medicine , only 20% of the worlds known plants have been examined for medicinal qualities . There is also the potential for new food crops to be discovered - Australia may soon have it 's first commercial native grain crop through the selective breeding of a native grass called Alpine rice ( Microlaena stipoides ) that is high in protein and gluten free . <p> Ronnie Vernooy/Bioversity International <h> What 's happening in Australia ? <p> Australia has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 23% of Australia 's plants are listed as endangered , one of the highest rates of endangerment in the world . <p> The Australian Seedbank Partnership ( ASBP ) brings together 14 different organisations including botanic gardens , state environment agencies , academic and non-government organisations . So far the partnership has secured one-third of Australia 's plants in conservation seed banks . <p> The fieldwork has led to the rediscovery of plants thought extinct , such as the Showy Violet ( Viola betonicifolia ) in South Australia , and translocation projects for plants like the chalky wattle . <p> The ASBP is working on a " 1000 species project " - collecting and banking the seed of 1000 rare and threatened , provincially endemic or economically important species from across Australia . <p> Through seed banking and associated germination trials , the " plants on the precipice " project aims to determine the germination requirements of montane and alpine plant species . This may prove invaluable for preserving species most exposed to the threats of climate change . <p> The ASBP is also focused on the protection of species susceptible @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Myrtle Rust . <p> One of the biggest issues seedbanks face in Australia and around the world is continual funding . Seed bank collections require long-term , ongoing curation . The facilities that house them need ongoing maintenance . As such , regular funding is required for the life of a seed bank and government support is of major importance . Support from State and Territory Governments vary . The New South Wales and to some extent Western Australian Governments provide excellent support for the partners from those states , where as support from the Queensland and Northern Territory Governments is virtually non-existent . Historically , support from the federal government has been somewhat limited , particularly in regards to the ongoing operation of the seed banks . <p> With the rising threats of climate change , over exploitation and land use change , it is becoming increasingly important to ensure seedbanks around the world are supported now and in the future .
